def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build connected components using union-find or by grouping
    # Group elements by their prime factors - elements that share any prime factor are connected
    
    # For each element, we'll find all prime factors it has
    # Two elements are connected if they share at least one prime factor
    
    # We can group by the union of prime factors
    # For each element, create a "signature" of its prime factors
    # Elements with overlapping prime factors can be swapped
    
    # Instead, let's create groups based on prime factors
    # For each prime factor, all elements having that factor are in the same group
    
    # Find all primes that appear in any number
    all_primes = set()
    for factors in prime_factors:
        all_primes.update(factors)
    
    # Group elements by which primes they have
    elements_groups = {}  # key: prime factor, value: list of indices that have this prime
    
    for prime in all_primes:
        elements_groups[prime] = []
    
    # Assign each element to which primes it has
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for prime in factors:
            elements_groups[prime].append(i)
    
    # Now we have multiple groups (one for each prime factor)
    # Elements in the same group can be freely rearranged
    
    # For each prime factor group, check if the multiset of elements in original array 
    # matches the multiset of elements in sorted array for that group
    
    # But we need to find connected components - actually, if two elements share any prime factor, 
    # they can be swapped, so all elements sharing at least one prime factor form a connected component
    
    # Let's build connected components using union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect elements that share prime factors
    # For each prime factor, connect all elements that have it
    for prime, indices in elements_groups.items():
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Now group elements by their connected component
    comp_group = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in comp_group:
            comp_group[root] = []
        comp_group[root].append(i)
    
    # For each connected component, check if the multiset of original elements 
    # matches the multiset of sorted elements
    for component in comp_group.values():
        # Get elements in original array for this component
        original_elements = [arr[i] for i in component]
        # Get elements in sorted array for this component
        sorted_elements = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if multisets are equal
        if sorted(original_elements) != sorted(sorted_elements):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The problem is about determining if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.

Key insights:
1. We can only swap two elements if they share a common prime factor
2. We need to determine if we can reach the sorted sequence through such swaps
3. This is essentially about connectivity - elements that can be connected through shared prime factors form connected components

## Key Insight

The crucial observation is that if we can swap elements that share common prime factors, then elements within the same connected component (connected by shared prime factors) can be rearranged arbitrarily among themselves.

So the question becomes: can we sort the sequence if we can freely rearrange elements within connected components?

The answer is "1" (coprime-sortable) if and only if for each connected component, the elements within it have the same multiset of values as in their sorted positions.

## Algorithm

1. Find all prime factors of each number in the sequence
2. Group elements by their shared prime factors - elements sharing at least one prime factor are in the same component
3. For each connected component, check if the multiset of elements in the original sequence matches the multiset of elements in the sorted sequence at those positions
4. If all components satisfy this condition, output "1", otherwise "0"

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Find prime factors**: For each number, find all its prime factors
2. **Build components**: Use union-find or simply group elements by their prime factors
3. **Check each component**: For each connected component, verify that the multiset of elements in the original sequence matches the multiset in the sorted sequence

Let me implement this:

```python
def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create sorted version
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_sets = []
    for num in a:
        prime_sets.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build connected components using union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect elements that share common prime factors
    # For each prime factor, group all elements that have this prime factor
    # and connect them
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    # Group indices by their prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        prime_sets[i] = tuple(sorted(prime_sets[i]))  # Normalize for hashing
        for prime in prime_sets[i]:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime factor, connect all indices that have this prime factor
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        # Connect all indices in this group
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Check if each component can be rearranged
    # For each component, check if original and sorted multisets match
    component_groups = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in component_groups:
            component_groups[root] = []
        component_groups[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, check if the multiset of values matches
    # between original and sorted sequence
    for component in component_groups.values():
        # Get values in original sequence at this component
        original_vals = [a[i] for i in component]
        # Get values in sorted sequence at this component
        sorted_vals = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if multisets are identical
        if sorted(original_vals) != sorted(sorted_vals):
            return "0"
    
    return "1"

print(solve())
```

```python
def union(x, y):
    px, py = find(x), find(y)
    if px != py:
        parent[px] = py

# Group indices by their prime factors
prime_to_indices = {}

for i in range(n):
    # For each prime factor of element at index i
    for prime in prime_sets[i]:
        if prime not in prime_to_indices:
            prime_to_indices[prime] = []
        prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)

# Connect all indices that share the same prime factor
for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
    # Connect all indices in this group
    for i in range(1, len(indices)):
        union(indices[0], indices[i])

# Check if each component can be rearranged
component_groups = {}
for i in range(n):
    root = find(i)
    if root not in component_groups:
        component_groups[root] = []
    component_groups[root].append(i)

# For each component, check if the multiset of values matches
for component in component_groups.values():
    # Get values in original sequence at this component
    original_vals = [a[i] for i in component]
    # Get values in sorted sequence at this component
    sorted_vals = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
    
    # Check if multisets are identical
    if sorted(original_vals) != sorted(sorted_vals):
        print("0")
        exit()

print("1")
```

def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.
2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.
3. We need to output 1 if coprime-sortable, 0 otherwise.

## Key Insight

This is essentially a graph connectivity problem:
- Each number can be connected to other numbers that share a prime factor
- Numbers in the same connected component can be rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be sortable, each number must be able to reach its final position in the sorted sequence

## Solution Approach

1. Find all prime factors of each number in the array
2. Group numbers by their shared prime factors (connected components)
3. For each connected component, check if all numbers in that component can reach their correct positions in the sorted array
4. If every number can reach its target position, return 1; otherwise 0

## Detailed Steps

1. **Prime Factorization**: For each number, find all its prime factors
2. **Union-Find/Connectivity**: Group numbers that share prime factors
3. **Position Check**: For each number, check if it belongs to the same connected component as the target number in the sorted array

## Algorithm

1. Create an array of the original sequence and sort it to get the target positions
2. For each number, find its prime factors
3. Use union-find or simply track connectivity through prime factors
4. For each position in the sorted array, check if the number at that position is in the same connected component as the original number that should go there

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create sorted array to know the target values
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Use union-find to group elements by shared prime factors
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect elements that share prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # If elements i and j share a prime factor, they can be swapped
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection of sets
                union(i, j)
    
    # Check if each element can reach its target position
    # For each position in sorted array, check if the original element
    # that should go there is in the same component as the element that is currently there
    
    # Create a mapping from value to original positions
    value_to_positions = {}
    for i, val in enumerate(arr):
        if val not in value_to_positions:
            value_to_positions[val] = []
        value_to_positions[val].append(i)
    
    # For each value, we need to check if each original position of that value
    # can reach the position it should go to in sorted array
    
    # Actually, simpler approach: check if for each value, all elements with that value
    # are in the same connected component (since they need to be able to reach each other's positions)
    
    # Check if all elements with the same value are in the same connected component
    # But we need to be more careful - the values might not be the same
    
    # Correct approach: For each position i in final sorted array, 
    # check if element arr[i] can reach the target value sorted_arr[i]
    
    # Actually, let's think differently:
    # Elements that share prime factors can be rearranged among themselves
    # So we need to check that for each position, the element that should go there
    # can reach that position via the connectivity graph
    
    # For each value, the positions where that value appears must be in the same component
    # because elements with the same value need to be able to be rearranged among themselves
    
    # But actually, multiple different values can be in the same component
    
    # The key insight: numbers in the same connected component can be rearranged freely
    # So we need to check that for every index i, the element arr[i] and the element 
    # sorted_arr[i] are in the same connected component?
    
    # No - that's not right. The element at position i in original array must be able to 
    # reach the position where it should go in the sorted array.
    
    # Actually, the answer is: for each value v, all positions where v appears must 
    # belong to the same connected component?
    
    # Let me reconsider with the examples:
    
    # Example 1: [6,4,2,3,7] -> sorted [2,3,4,6,7]
    # Prime factors: 6->{2,3}, 4->{2}, 2->{2}, 3->{3}, 7->{7}
    # Components: {0,1,2} (all share factor 2), {3} (only shares 3), {4} (only 7)
    # In sorted array: 2,3,4,6,7
    # So 2 (original at pos 2) goes to pos 0, 3 (original at pos 3) goes to pos 1, etc.
    
    # Element 6 (at pos 0) must go to pos 3. Can it? 6 shares factor 2 with elements at 1,2,0.
    # So position 0,1,2 are connected. But 6 needs to go to position 3, and 3 is only connected through 3 (prime 3).
    # 6 has prime factors {2,3}, so it shares factor 3 with 3 at position 3.
    # Therefore, 6 and 3 can be swapped, so they are in the same component.
    
    # Actually, I need to connect any two elements that share a prime factor.
    # So elements that share at least one prime factor are connected.
    
    # So for each pair of elements that share a prime factor, union them.
    
    # After union-find, we have connected components.
    # Now, for the sequence to be sortable:
    # For each value in the final sorted array, the element that should be at that position 
    # must exist in a connected component that contains the original elements that need to reach there.
    
    # Actually, simpler: since we can rearrange within connected components, 
    # for the sequence to be sortable, the elements that need to be in certain positions 
    # must be able to move through connected components to reach those positions.
    
    # The key insight is that the relative ordering of elements in different connected components 
    # might not matter. But actually, we can only swap elements that share a prime factor.
    
    # Actually, the correct insight is: elements in the same connected component can be freely permuted.
    # So we need to check that for each value v, all occurrences of v are in the same connected component?
    # No, that's not right because different values can be in the same component.
    
    # Actually, the answer is that the sequence is coprime-sortable if and only if 
    # for every value v, all the elements with value v are in the same connected component.
    
    # Wait, no - look at example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] -> sorted [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
    
    # Let's trace: 
    # 2: prime factors {2}
    # 9: prime factors {3} 
    # 6: prime factors {2,3}
    # 5: prime factors {5}
    # 6: prime factors {2,3}
    # 7: prime factors {7}
    # 3: prime factors {3}
    
    # Components:
    # 2 ({2}) - only connected to 6 (shares 2), 6 (shares 2), and 6 (shares 2)
    # 9 ({3}) - connected to 6 (shares 3), 6 (shares 3), 3 (shares 3)
    # 5 ({5}) - isolated
    # 7 ({7}) - isolated
    # 3 ({3}) - connected to 6,9
    
    # So component 0 (2): positions where 2 appears -> should check if 2 can go to where it should
    # But actually, the values 2,3,5,6,6,7,9 need to be sorted.
    
    # The key insight from known similar problems: 
    # Two elements can be swapped if they share a prime factor, so they belong to the same connected component.
    # Therefore, the values that appear in the same connected component can be freely rearranged.
    
    # For the sequence to be sorted, we need that for any two positions i and j, 
    # if the values at those positions are in different connected components, 
    # then they cannot be swapped, so the sorting would fail if these values need to be 
    # in different orders.
    
    # Actually, more precisely: the sorting is possible if and only if for every pair of values 
    # v1 and v2, if v1 appears in the sorted array at position i and v2 at position j 
    # (where v1 < v2), and v1 and v2 belong to different connected components, 
    # then we cannot swap them to get v1 to position i and v2 to position j.
    
    # But this is too complex.
    
    # Known result: This is equivalent to grouping numbers by their prime factors and 
    # checking if the multiset of values in each connected component can be rearranged.
    
    # Actually, the correct approach is: 
    # 1. Group indices by connected components (using union-find with prime factors)
    # 2. For each connected component, collect all values at those indices
    # 3. In the sorted array, check if the values in each connected component can be arranged 
    #    in increasing order.
    
    # But that's not quite right either, because values can be rearranged freely within components.
    
    # The insight is: since elements within a connected component can be freely permuted, 
    # the only constraint is that for each connected component, the multiset of values 
    # in that component must appear in the sorted array in a way that respects the component.
    
    # Actually, simpler: for each position in the sorted array, the value should be one 
    # that appears in the connected component of the original position that contains the element 
    # that should go there.
    
    # But we can actually do this: 
    # For each connected component, let's collect all the values that appear in indices 
    # of that component. In the final sorted array, for each position, we need that the value 
    # at that position is one that belongs to the same component as the original position.
    
    # Actually, the values that can be moved are constrained by the components.
    # So if a value v appears in the sorted array at position i, and the original position 
    # of that value (or any position where v appears) is in component C, then all values 
    # that should be in positions in component C must be from values that appear in component C.
    
    # Actually, the known solution for this problem is:
    # 1. Find all prime factors for each element
    # 2. Use union-find to group indices that share at least one prime factor
    # 3. For each component, check that all values in that component can be sorted within themselves
    
    # But since we can rearrange freely within components, the only issue is if the values 
    # in a component don't have the right values for their positions.
    
    # Actually, since we can rearrange within components freely, the values at the final 
    # positions must be from the values that originally appeared in the same component.
    
    # So for each connected component C (with indices S), we look at the values at those indices, 
    # and in the sorted array, we check that the values that appear in positions corresponding 
    # to these indices can be formed from the original values in C.
    
    # More precisely: for each position in the sorted array, we need to check that if the element 
    # at that position has a value v, then there must be some original position from component C 
    # that had value v.
    
    # Actually, we need to check that the multiset of values in the sorted array for positions 
    # in component C matches exactly with the multiset of values in the original array for positions in C.
    
    # But that's not sufficient because the values might be distributed differently.
    
    # Final correct insight: 
    # Since we can permute elements within each connected component freely, 
    # the sorting is possible if and only if for each connected component, 
    # the set of values that appear in the sorted array at positions that are in that component 
    # is exactly the same as the set of values that appear in the original array at positions in that component.
    
    # Actually, that's not quite right either - we need the multiset to be the same.
    
    # The correct approach is:
    # 1. Group indices by connected components using prime factors
    # 2. For each component, get the values at those indices in the original array
    # 3. In the sorted array, get the values at those same indices (or positions in the component)
    # 4. The sorting is possible if for each component, the multiset of values in the original 
    #    array for that component equals the multiset of values in the sorted array 
    #    for the positions in that component.
    
    # But the component doesn't have fixed positions in the sorted array.
    
    # Actually, let me think differently: 
    # For a sequence to be coprime-sortable, it must be possible to rearrange elements 
    # in each connected component to match the sorted order of those values.
    
    # Since we can freely rearrange elements within a connected component, 
    # we can achieve the sorted order for that component if and only if 
    # the values in the sorted array that belong to that component (i.e., the values that have 
    # their original positions in the component) can be arranged to match the sorted order.
    
    # But actually, we can put any value from the component into any position within that component.
    
    # Therefore, for each connected component C (with set of indices S), 
    # in the sorted array, the values that appear in positions i where i is in S must 
    # be exactly the values that appear in the original array at positions in S.
    
    # This is the key insight!
    
    # For each connected component C:
    # - Let A be the original values at positions in C
    # - Let B be the sorted array values at positions in C (where we consider the same set of positions)
    # - Then we need A and B to have the same multiset.
    
    # But this assumes we're looking at the same set of positions.
    
    # Actually, we need to check that for any permutation within the component, 
    # the values can be arranged in sorted order.
    
    # Since we can rearrange arbitrarily within a component (because connectivity allows any swaps), 
    # the values in the component can be rearranged in any order. 
    # So the only constraint is that the values in the sorted array for positions in the component 
    # must be exactly the values that were originally in the component.
    
    # So algorithm:
    # 1. Find connected components of indices using prime factors
    # 2. For each component, get the values in original array at indices in that component
    # 3. Get the values in sorted array at the same indices (positions from 0 to n-1)
    # 4. Check if the sorted multiset of values in original component equals sorted multiset of values in sorted component
    
    # But the positions in the component are fixed by their index.
    
    # Let me verify with example 1:
    # Original: [6,4,2,3,7], sorted: [2,3,4,6,7]
    # Index 0,1,2,3,4
    # Prime factors: 6->{2,3}, 4->{2}, 2->{2}, 3->{3}, 7->{7}
    # Components: 
    # 0 and 1: share factor 2 -> connect 0 and 1
    # 0 and 2: share factor 2 -> connect 0,1,2
    # 3: only prime factor 3, no other shares 3, so isolated
    # 4: only prime factor 7, isolated
    # 
    # Actually, 6 (index 0) and 3 (index 3) share factor 3! So 0 and 3 should be connected.
    # 6 and 3 both have factor 3.
    # 4 (index 1) has factor 2, shares with 0,2
    # 2 (index 2) has factor 2, shares with 0,1
    # 3 (index 3) has factor 3, shares with 6 (index 0)
    # 7 (index 4) has factor 7, no one else has 7
    
    # So component 1: indices 0,1,2,3 (share 2 or 3)
    # Component 2: index 4 (only 7)
    
    # Component 1: original values: [6,4,2,3] -> multiset {2,3,4,6}
    # Component 1 sorted array values at positions 0,1,2,3: [2,3,4,6] -> multiset {2,3,4,6}
    # Component 2: original: [7], sorted: [7] -> multiset {7}
    # So both match. Answer is 1.
    
    # Example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3] -> sorted [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
    # Values: 2,9,6,5,6,7,3
    # Prime factors:
    # 2: {2}
    # 9: {3}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 5: {5}
    # 6: {2,3}
    # 7: {7}
    # 3: {3}
    
    # Pairs sharing prime factors:
    # 2 and 6 -> share 2
    # 6 and 9 -> share 3
    # 6 and 6 -> share 2,3
    # 6 and 3 -> share 3
    # 9 and 3 -> share 3
    
    # Components:
    # 2 shares with 6, so 0 and 2,3,4 (indices of 6s) are connected
    # 9 shares with 6,3, so 1,2,3,4,6 are connected
    # 5 is isolated
    # 7 is isolated
    
    # So indices: 0,1,2,3,4,5,6
    # Component 1: 0,2,3,4,6 (2,6,6,6,3) - values: [2,6,6,6,3]
    # Component 2: 5 (5) - value [5]
    # Component 3: 1 (9) - value [9]
    
    # In sorted array: [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
    # Component 1 values at indices 0,2,3,4,6: 
    #   sorted value at 0: 2, 2:3, 3:6, 4:6, 6:9 -> values [2,3,6,6,9]
    # Original values in component: [2,6,6,6,3]
    # Multisets: {2,3,6,6} vs {2,3,6,6,9} - different!
    # So answer is 0.
    
    # But the sorted array at indices 0,2,3,4,6 gives values 2,3,6,6,9
    # The values in the component should be 2,3,6,6,6 (original)
    # But 9 is in the sorted array at index 6, which is in component 1, 
    # but the original array had no 9 at indices 0,2,3,4,6. The 9 was at index 1.
    
    # Actually, the 9 at index 1 is in component 1, so in the sorted array, 
    # the value 9 should appear at some position in component 1.
    
    # Since we can move elements freely within component 1, we should be able to 
    # arrange the values {2,3,6,6,6} in the component positions in sorted order.
    # But the sorted array requires values {2,3,5,6,6,7,9}.
    
    # The component 1 has values: 2,6,6,6,3 -> which is {2,3,6,6,6}
    # The sorted array has {2,3,5,6,6,7,9} - so in component 1 positions, we have {2,3,6,6,9}
    # But 9 is not in the original component values! It's missing from original component 1.
    # The original component 1 has values from positions 0,2,3,4,6: values 2,6,6,6,3
    # Values: 2,6,6,6,3 - no 9.
    # So we cannot have 9 in any position of component 1 because it wasn't originally there.
    
    # Therefore, the algorithm is:
    # 1. Build connected components of indices using prime factors
    # 2. For each component C (set of indices S):
    #    a. Get original values: values_original = [arr[i] for i in S]
    #    b. Get sorted values at positions in S: values_sorted = [sorted_arr[i] for i in S]
    #    c. Check if sorted(values_original) == sorted(values_sorted)
    # 3. If all components pass, return 1, else 0
    
    # But wait - if the sorted array has a value that isn't in the original component values, 
    # then we can't achieve it.
    
    # Actually, we need the multiset of values in the component to be exactly the same in both the original and sorted arrays for those positions.
    
    # Let's implement this.

    # First, build union-find and find components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each pair of indices, if they share a prime factor, union them
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if prime_factors[i] & prime_factors[j]:  # intersection is non-empty
                union(i, j)
    
    # Group indices by component
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Check each component
    for component_indices in components.values():
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in component_indices]
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component_indices]
        
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, this is O(n^2) in the worst case for the nested loops. With n up to 10^5, this would be 10^10 operations which is too slow.

We need to optimize the prime factor union-find.

## Optimized Approach

Instead of checking all pairs, we can:
1. For each prime factor, collect all indices that have that prime factor
2. Union all indices that share the same prime factor

This reduces complexity from O(n^2) to O(n * number_of_prime_factors).

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted array to know the target values
sorted_arr = sorted(arr)

# Find prime factors for each element
prime_factors = []
for num in arr:
    prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))

# Use union-find to group indices that share a prime factor
parent = list(range(n))

def find(x):
    if parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = find(parent[x])
    return parent[x]

def union(x, y):
    px, py = find(x), find(y)
    if px != py:
        parent[px] = py

# Group indices by prime factors - for each prime, union all indices that have it
# We'll create a set of primes that appear
all_primes = set()
for factors in prime_factors:
    all_primes.update(factors)

# For each prime, get the indices that have it
for prime in all_primes:
    prime_indices = []
    for i in range(n):
        if prime in prime_factors[i]:
            prime_indices.append(i)
    
    # If more than one index has this prime, union them
    if len(prime_indices) > 1:
        for i in range(1, len(prime_indices)):
            union(prime_indices[0], prime_indices[i])

# Group indices by component
components = {}
for i in range(n):
    root = find(i)
    if root not in components:
        components[root] = []
    components[root].append(i)

# Check each component
for component_indices in components.values():
    original_values = [arr[i] for i in component_indices]
    sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in component_indices]
    
    if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
        print(0)
        exit()

print(1)
```

def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create sorted array to know the target values
sorted_arr = sorted(arr)

# Find prime factors for each element
prime_factors = []
for num in arr:
    prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))

# Use union-find to group indices that share a prime factor
parent = list(range(n))

def find(x):
    if parent[x] != x:
        parent[x] = find(parent[x])
    return parent[x]

def union(x, y):
    px, py = find(x), find(y)
    if px != py:
        parent[px] = py

# Group indices by prime factors - for each prime, union all indices that have it
# We'll create a set of primes that appear
all_primes = set()
for factors in prime_factors:
    all_primes.update(factors)

# For each prime, get the indices that have it
for prime in all_primes:
    prime_indices = []
    for i in range(n):
        if prime in prime_factors[i]:
            prime_indices.append(i)
    
    # If more than one index has this prime, union them
    if len(prime_indices) > 1:
        for i in range(1, len(prime_indices)):
            union(prime_indices[0], prime_indices[i])

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The problem is about determining if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.

Key insights:
- We can only swap two elements if they share a common prime factor
- The goal is to determine if we can reach the sorted order through such swaps
- This is essentially asking: are the elements in the same "connected component" of the graph where nodes are numbers and edges connect numbers with common prime factors?

## Key Insight

This becomes a graph connectivity problem:
- Each number from the sequence is a node
- Two numbers are connected if they share a prime factor
- Elements in the same connected component can be rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, elements in each connected component must already be in their correct relative positions in the final sorted order

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Find prime factors** of all numbers in the sequence
2. **Group numbers** by their prime factors - numbers connected through shared primes
3. **For each connected component**, check if the elements in that component are already in the correct sorted positions
4. **If all components have their elements in the right relative order**, return 1; otherwise return 0

## Detailed Approach

1. For each number, find all its prime factors
2. Create a graph where numbers are nodes and edges exist between numbers with common prime factors
3. Find connected components using union-find or DFS/BFS
4. For each connected component, extract the elements and sort them
5. Check if in the original array, each element of the component appears in the same relative order as in the sorted version

## Implementation

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Group indices by their prime factors using union-find
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect indices that share prime factors
    # For each prime, connect all numbers that have this prime factor
    primes_used = set()
    
    # Create a mapping from prime to list of indices that have this prime
    prime_to_indices = {}
    
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all indices that have this prime
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Group indices by their root parent
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)

        #

        #

        #

        #

        # 
        # The correct approach:
        # 1. For each connected component, collect the values at the indices in that component
        # 2. Sort these values
        # 3. For the array to be coprime-sortable, the sorted values in each component must appear in the 
        #    positions that correspond to the indices in that component in the sorted array.
        #

        # 
        # Actually, we need to check that if we sort the values in each component, and then place them in the 
        # positions of that component, it matches the sorted array.
        # 
        # Algorithm:
        # 1. Group indices by connected components using union-find with prime factors
        # 2. For each component, get the values at those indices
        # 3. Sort these values
        # 4. In the sorted array, extract the values at the same positions (in the sorted array, the values should

        # - Create a sorted array
        # - For each component, take the set of indices in that component in the original array
        # - In the sorted array, take the values at those same indices (but the sorted array is sorted by value)
        # - Extract the values from the sorted array at the positions that correspond to the original positions in the component
        # 
        # Actually, we need to match by the values, not by positions.
        # 
        # We can do:
        # 1. For each component, get the values at its indices in the original array
        # 2. Sort these values
        # 3. In the sorted array (which is sorted by value), take the values that belong to the same component
        #    in the sorted array
        # 
        # But how do we assign values to components in the sorted array?
        # 
        # Since we can permute within a component, we can achieve any ordering of values within a component.
        # Therefore, we can achieve the sorted array if and only if for each connected component, the values
        # in that component can be arranged in non-decreasing order.
        # 
        # But that's always possible, so the answer should be 1.
        # 
        # I found a different interpretation: the values must be able to reach their final positions by swapping.
        # For a value to reach its final position, there must be a path of swaps that allows it to move.
        # 
        # For a value at index i to reach index j in the sorted array, there must be a sequence of swaps
        # that allows it to move from i to j, and during this sequence, the values can only be swapped if they share
        # a common prime factor.
        # 
        # This means that the value at index i can only move to other positions that are in the same connected component
        # as index i.
        # 
        # In the sorted array, the value that should be at index j must be in the same connected component as the value
        # at index i.
        # 
        # Therefore, the condition is: for each value, in the sorted array, the value that should be at position i
        # must be in the same connected component as the value currently at position i.
        # 
        # This is the key insight!
        # 
        # Algorithm:
        # 1. Find connected components of indices based on shared prime factors
        # 2. For each index i in the original array, find which component it belongs to
        # 3. In the sorted array, find the value that should be at position i
        # 4. Check that this value belongs to the same component as the value at position i in the original array
        # 
        # More precisely:
        # - Let sorted_arr be the sorted version of the original array
        # - For each index i from 0 to n-1:
        #   - Original value at i is arr[i]
        #   - Sorted value at i is sorted_arr[i]
        #   - The value sorted_arr[i] should be "reachable" from arr[i]
        #   - That is, there must be a path from the component of arr[i] to the component of sorted_arr[i]
        # 
        # Since we can swap within a component, and values can only move within their component, the value at position i
        # can only move to positions in the same connected component.
        # 
        # Therefore, if the value that should be at position i in the sorted array is not in the same component as the value
        # currently at position i, then it's impossible.
        # 
        # But the value at position i in the original array is arr[i], and in the sorted array, the value at position i is 
        # sorted_arr[i]. This value sorted_arr[i] must be in the same component as arr[i] in the original array.
        # 
        # Actually, we need to ensure that the value sorted_arr[i] is in the same component as the value arr[i].
        # 
        # Let me verify with the examples:
        # 
        # Example 1: [6,4,2,3,7]
        # sorted_arr = [2,3,4,6,7]
        # Component of each index:
        # - index 0 (6): prime factors {2,3} -> component with 2,3,4,6
        # - index 1 (4): prime factors {2} -> in the same component (since 4 has prime 2, and 2,6 have prime 2)
        # - index 2 (2): prime factors {2} -> same component
        # - index 3 (3): prime factors {3} -> in the same component (3 and 6 have prime 3)
        # - index 4 (7): prime factors {7} -> alone
        # 
        # Check each position:
        # - i=0: value in original is 6, in sorted is 2. 
        #   Component of 6: {0,1,2,3}, component of 2: {0,1,2,3} - same component
        # - i=1: value in original is 4, in sorted is 3. 
        #   Component of 4: {0,1,2,3}, component of 3: {0,1,2,3} - same
        # - i=2: value in original is 2, in sorted is 4.
        #   Component of 2: {0,1,2,3}, component of 4: {0,1,2,3} - same
        # - i=3: value in original is 3, in sorted is 6.
        #   Component of 3: {0,1,2,3}, component of 6: {0,1,2,3} - same
        # - i=4: value in original is 7, in sorted is 7.
        #   Component of 7: {4}, component of 7: {4} - same
        # 
        # So answer is 1 - correct.
        # 
        # Example 2: [2,9,6,5,6,7,3]
        # sorted_arr = [2,3,5,6,6,7,9]
        # 
        # First, find connected components:
        # - 2: prime {2}
        # - 9: prime {3}
        # - 6: prime {2,3}
        # - 5: prime {5}
        # - 6: prime {2,3}
        # - 7: prime {7}
        # - 3: prime {3}
        # 
        # Components:
        # - Component 1: indices with prime 2 or 3: indices 0,1,2,3,4,5,6? 
        #   - Index 0 (2) has prime 2
        #   - Index 1 (9) has prime 3
        #   - Index 2 (6) has both
        #   - Index 3 (5) has only 5
        #   - Index 4 (6) has both
        #   - Index 5 (7) has only 7
        #   - Index 6 (3) has only 3
        # 
        # So components:
        # - {0,1,2,4,6} - because they have prime 2 or 3
        # - {3} - only prime 5
        # - {5} - only prime 7
        # 
        # Now check each position:
        # - i=0: original value=2, sorted value=2. 
        #   Component of 2: {0,1,2,4,6}, component of 2: same - good
        # - i=1: original value=9, sorted value=3.
        #   Component of 9: {0,1,2,4,6}, component of 3: {0,1,2,4,6} - same - good
        # - i=2: original value=6, sorted value=5.
        #   Component of 6: {0,1,2,4,6}, component of 5: {3} - different! 
        #   This is bad.
        # - i=3: original value=5, sorted value=6.
        #   Component of 5: {3}, component of 6: {0,1,2,4,6} - different - bad
        # - i=4: original value=6, sorted value=6.
        #   Component of 6: {0,1,2,4,6}, component of 6: same - good
        # - i=5: original value=7, sorted value=7.
        #   Component of 7: {5}, component of 7: same - good
        # - i=6: original value=3, sorted value=9.
        #   Component of 3: {0,1,2,4,6}, component of 9: {0,1,2,4,6} - same - good
        # 
        # At positions 2 and 3, we have a mismatch.
        # 
        # Therefore, answer is 0 - correct.
        # 
        # So the algorithm is:
        # 1. Get prime factors for each number
        # 2. Use union-find to group indices that share any prime factor
        # 3. For each index i, determine which component it belongs to
        # 4. Create sorted array
        # 5. For each index i, check that the value at i in sorted array belongs to the same component as the value at i in original array
        # 6. If all positions satisfy this, return 1, else 0
        
        # Implementation:
        
        # Step 1: get prime factors
        prime_factors = [get_prime_factors(num) for num in arr]
        
        # Step 2: union-find to group indices by shared prime factors
        parent = list(range(n))
        
        def find(x):
            if parent[x] != x:
                parent[x] = find(parent[x])
            return parent[x]
        
        def union(x, y):
            px, py = find(x), find(y)
            if px != py:
                parent[px] = py
        
        # For each prime, connect all indices that have this prime factor
        # We need to group by prime factors
        prime_to_indices = {}
        for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
            for prime in factors:
                if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                    prime_to_indices[prime] = []
                prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
        
        # For each prime, union all indices that have this prime
        for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
            for j in range(1, len(indices)):
                union(indices[0], indices[j])
        
        # Step 3: create sorted array
        sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
        
        # Step 4: for each index, check component match
        for i in range(n):
            original_val = arr[i]
            sorted_val = sorted_arr[i]
            
            # Find component of original_val
            # The value original_val is at index i, so we need to find which component i belongs to
            original_component = find(i)
            
            # Find which component contains value sorted_val
            # We need to find an index j such that arr[j] == sorted_val and find(j) == original_component
            # But wait, the value sorted_val might appear multiple times
            # We need to check if there exists an index j such that:
            #   arr[j] == sorted_val and find(j) == original_component
            # But actually, we need to check that the value sorted_val can come from the component of i
            # Specifically, we need to find if there is some index j in the same component as i such that arr[j] == sorted_val
            
            # Find the component of the value sorted_val
            # Since the value sorted_val is in the sorted array at position i, it was originally at some position j
            # with value sorted_val.
            # We need to check if j is in the same component as i.
            
            # Find all positions where the value is sorted_val in the original array
            positions_with_sorted_val = []
            for j in range(n):
                if arr[j] == sorted_val:
                    positions_with_sorted_val.append(j)
            
            # Check if any of these positions is in the same component as i
            component_of_i = find(i)
            found_in_same_component = False
            for j in positions_with_sorted_val:
                if find(j) == component_of_i:
                    found_in_same_component = True
                    break
            
            if not found_in_same_component:
                return 0
        
        return 1

print(solve())
```

Wait, I see a problem in my implementation. I need to find which component contains the value `sorted_val` across all positions in the original array.

Actually, the value `sorted_val` appears at some positions in the original array. For the value to be able to move to position `i` in the sorted array, there must be a position `j` where:
- `arr[j] == sorted_val`
- `find(j) == find(i)` (same component)

So I need to check if such a position exists.

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors for each number
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Union-find for indices
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Group indices by shared prime factors
    prime_to_indices = {}
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all indices that have it
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        for j in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[j])
    
    # Create sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # For each position i in sorted array, check if the value can be moved from its original position to i
    for i in range(n):
        original_val = arr[i]
        sorted_val = sorted_arr[i]
        
        # Find component of current position i
        component_of_i = find(i)
        
        # Find if there's any position j in the original array with value sorted_val that is in the same component as i
        found_in_same_component = False
        for j in range(n):
            if arr[j] == sorted_val and find(j) == component_of_i:
                found_in_same_component = True
                break
        
        if not found_in_same_component:
            return 0
    
    return 1

print(solve())
```

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Find prime factors for each number
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Union-find for indices
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Group indices by shared prime factors
    prime_to_indices = {}
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for prime in factors:
            if prime not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[prime] = []
            prime_to_indices[prime].append(i)
    
    # For each prime, union all indices that have it
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

2. Two elements can be swapped if they share at least one prime factor.

The key insight is that if two numbers share a prime factor, they are in the same "connected component" of the graph where nodes are numbers and edges represent shared prime factors.

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get the sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build connected components - group indices by shared prime factors
    # For each index, we get its prime factors
    # Two indices are connected if they share a common prime factor
    
    # Create a mapping from prime factor to indices that have that factor
    prime_to_indices = {}
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[factor] = []
            prime_to_indices[factor].append(i)
    
    # Now find connected components - all indices that share at least one common prime factor
    # We can use union-find or just find all groups connected through prime factors
    
    # Since prime_to_indices gives us all indices for each prime, we can find connected components
    # using the fact that if two indices have a common prime factor, they are connected
    
    # Use union-find to group indices
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each prime factor, union all indices that have that factor
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Now group indices by their root parent
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, get the original values and sorted values
    # Check if the multiset of values in each component matches in original and sorted array
    
    # For each component in the original array
    for comp_indices in components.values():
        # Get values in this component in original array
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in comp_indices]
        original_multiset = {}
        for val in original_values:
            original_multiset[val] = original_multiset.get(val, 0) + 1
        
        # Get values in this component in sorted array
        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in comp_indices]
        sorted_multiset = {}
        for val in sorted_values:
            sorted_multiset[val] = sorted_multiset.get(val, 0) + 1
        
        if original_multiset != sorted_multiset:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

solve()
```

def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get the sorted array
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr)
    
    # Find prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in arr:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build connected components - group indices by shared prime factors
    # For each index, we get its prime factors
    # Two indices are connected if they share a common prime factor
    
    # Create a mapping from prime factor to indices that have that factor
    prime_to_indices = {}
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for factor in factors:
            if factor not in prime_to_indices:
                prime_to_indices[factor] = []
            prime_to_indices[factor].append(i)
    
    # Now find connected components - all indices that share at least one common prime factor
    # We can use union-find or just find all groups connected through prime factors
    
    # Since prime_to_indices gives us all indices for each prime, we can find connected components
    # using the fact that if two indices have a common prime factor, they are connected
    
    # Use union-find to group indices
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # For each prime factor, union all indices that have that factor
    for prime, indices in prime_to_indices.items():
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Now group indices by their root parent
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # For each component, get the original values and sorted values
    # Check if the multiset of values in each component matches in original and sorted array
    
    # For each component in the original array
    for comp_indices in components.values():
        # Get values in this component in original array
        original_values = [arr[i] for i in comp_indices]
        original_multiset = {}
        for val in original_values:
            original_multiset[val] = original_multiset.get(val, 0) + 1

        sorted_values = [sorted_arr[i] for i in comp_indices]
        sorted_multiset = {}
        for val in sorted_values:

        if original_multiset != sorted_multiset:
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are now examining a unique method to sort a sequence of numbers in increasing order. The method only allows swapping of two numbers that have a common prime factor. For example, a sequence [6, 4, 2, 3, 7] can be sorted using the following steps.
Step 0: 6 4 2 3 7 (given sequence)
Step 1: 2 4 6 3 7 (elements 6 and 2 swapped)
Step 2: 2 6 4 3 7 (elements 4 and 6 swapped)
Step 3: 2 3 4 6 7 (elements 6 and 3 swapped)


Depending on the nature of the sequence, however, this approach may fail to complete the sorting. You have given a name "Coprime sort" to this approach and are now examining if a given sequence is coprime-sortable.

Make a program to determine if a given sequence can be sorted in increasing order by iterating an arbitrary number of swapping operations of two elements that have a common prime number.



Input

The input is given in the following format.


$N$
$a_1$ $a_2$ $...$ $a_N$


The first line provides the number of elements included in the sequence $N$ ($2 \leq N \leq 10^5$). The second line provides an array of integers $a_i$ ($2 \leq a_i \leq 10^5$) that constitute the sequence.

Output

Output "1" if the sequence is coprime-sortable in increasing order, or "0" otherwise.

Examples

Input

5
6 4 2 3 7


Output

1


Input

7
2 9 6 5 6 7 3


Output

0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of numbers and want to sort it in increasing order
2. We can only swap two elements if they have a common prime factor
3. We need to determine if the sequence is "coprime-sortable" (can be sorted using these allowed swaps)

## Key Insight

This is a graph connectivity problem. Think of it this way:

- Each number can be connected to other numbers that share at least one prime factor
- Numbers that are connected through a chain of common prime factors can be freely rearranged among themselves
- For the sequence to be sortable, all numbers that belong to the same connected component (same prime factor chain) must be able to reach their correct positions in the sorted sequence

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Find prime factors** of each number in the sequence
2. **Group numbers** that share common prime factors
3. **Check connectivity** - numbers that share prime factors can be swapped
4. **For sorting to be possible**: within each connected component of numbers that share prime factors, the relative ordering of values must be consistent with their positions in the sorted sequence

Actually, let me refine this:

The key insight is that if two numbers have a common prime factor, they can be swapped. This creates a connected graph where nodes are positions and edges exist between positions that have numbers with a common prime factor.

However, a simpler approach: two elements can be swapped if they share a prime factor. This means elements that are linked through prime factors can be rearranged freely.

So the algorithm is:
1. Find all prime factors of each number
2. Create a graph where positions are connected if the numbers at those positions share a prime factor
3. Find connected components of positions
4. For each connected component, check if the values in that component can be rearranged to their correct sorted positions

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me think again.

Actually, the numbers themselves can be moved around freely within their connected component. So if we have a component of positions that can be reached through swaps, then the values at those positions can be rearranged among themselves in any order.

Therefore, for the sequence to be sortable:
- For each connected component of positions (where positions are connected by numbers sharing prime factors), the values at those positions must be able to be rearranged to their correct sorted values.

But we don't need to check the exact rearrangement - we just need to check that for each connected component, the multiset of values in that component matches the multiset of values that should be in those positions after sorting.

## Final Algorithm

1. Find all prime factors for each number
2. Build a graph where positions are connected if the numbers at those positions share a prime factor
3. Find connected components
4. For each connected component of positions, collect the values at those positions
5. For the sequence to be coprime-sortable, the multiset of values in each component must match the multiset of values that should be in those positions after sorting

```python
def prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Create sorted array for reference
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # Find prime factors for each position
    prime_sets = []
    for num in a:
        prime_sets.append(prime_factors(num))
    
    # Build graph: positions are connected if they share a prime factor
    # Use union find to find connected components
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect positions that have numbers with common prime factors
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if len(prime_sets[i] & prime_sets[j]) > 0:  # They share at least one prime factor
                union(i, j)
    
    # Find all connected components
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Check if the sequence can be sorted
    # For each component, the values at those positions must be able to be rearranged
    # to the values that should be at those positions in sorted order
    
    for component in components.values():
        # Get values in this component
        values_in_component = [a[i] for i in component]
        # Get values that should be in this component (in sorted array)
        values_in_sorted = [sorted_a[i] for i in component]
        
        # Check if both multisets are equal
        if sorted(values_in_component) != sorted(values_in_sorted):
            return "0"
    
    return "1"

print(solve())
```

1. We need to determine if a sequence can be sorted in increasing order by swapping elements that have a common prime factor.

## Key Insight

The problem reduces to: can all elements be rearranged to their correct positions in sorted order?

## Approach

1. **Find all prime factors** of each number in the array.
2. **Group numbers** by their prime factors - numbers that share any prime factor can be rearranged among themselves.
3. **Check if each group can reach its target position** in the sorted sequence.

1. Find all prime factors for each element

The answer is yes if and only if for every connected component, the multiset of elements in that component in the original array matches the multiset of elements in that component in the sorted array.

## Final Algorithm

1. Find the prime factorization of each element
2. For each element, determine its prime factors (a set)
3. Group elements by their prime factors - create connected components

Actually, simpler:

We can sort the sequence. Then, we need to check if we can achieve this sorted order through swaps.

Two elements can swap if they share a prime factor.

So: elements that share a prime factor can be rearranged freely among themselves.

Therefore, for the sequence to be coprime-sortable:
- For every set of values that share a common prime factor, the elements with those values in the original array can be rearranged to their target positions in the sorted array.

The key is: elements that can be swapped must be able to reach their target positions.

But since swaps are transitive, all elements sharing a prime factor form a connected component that can be rearranged freely.

So: for each connected component (all elements that share any prime factor with each other through a chain), the elements in that component can be permuted freely.

Therefore, we need to check that for each connected component, the elements that appear in that component in the sorted array are exactly the same as in the original array.

Wait, that's not quite right either.

Actually, we need to check if the elements that belong to each connected component in the original sequence are exactly the same as the elements that belong to that component in the sorted sequence.

But since we're sorting, the values are fixed - it's about which values belong to which components.

So the algorithm:

1. Find prime factors of each element
2. For each element, find its prime factors
3. Build connected components based on shared prime factors
4. For each connected component, get the values in the original array and in the sorted array
5. Check if the multiset of values in the component is the same in both arrays

```python
def get_prime_factors(n):
    factors = set()
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        while n % d == 0:
            factors.add(d)
            n //= d
        d += 1
    if n > 1:
        factors.add(n)
    return factors

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Get prime factors for each element
    prime_factors = []
    for num in a:
        prime_factors.append(get_prime_factors(num))
    
    # Create a mapping from element to its prime factors
    # For each prime factor, we'll group elements that have it
    
    # Build connected components - elements that share a prime factor
    # We'll use union-find or just group by prime factors
    
    # For each prime factor, collect all elements that have it
    prime_to_elements = {}
    for i, factors in enumerate(prime_factors):
        for p in factors:
            if p not in prime_to_elements:
                prime_to_elements[p] = []
            prime_to_elements[p].append(i)
    
    # Also get the sorted array
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    
    # For each connected component (group of indices that share a prime factor),
    # we need to check if the multiset of values in the original array at those indices
    # matches the multiset of values in the sorted array at those indices.
    
    # But actually, we need to check if we can reach the sorted order.
    # Since elements in the same connected component can be rearranged freely,
    # the values that end up in positions of the component in the sorted array
    # must be those that were originally in the component.
    
    # Instead, let's think: for the sorted order to be achievable,
    # for each set of positions that are in the same connected component
    # (meaning elements sharing prime factors), the values in those positions
    # in the original array must be exactly the same as in the sorted array
    
    # Actually, we need to check: after sorting, are the elements that belong
    # to each connected component the same set?
    
    # Alternative approach: check if for every prime factor, 
    # the multiset of values in positions that have elements with this prime factor
    # is the same in original and sorted arrays.
    
    # But connected components are defined by shared prime factors.
    
    # Let me try a different approach: use union-find to connect indices
    # that can be swapped directly or indirectly.
    
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Connect indices that share a prime factor
    for prime, indices in prime_to_elements.items():
        # For each prime, connect all indices that have this prime factor
        for i in range(1, len(indices)):
            union(indices[0], indices[i])
    
    # Now all indices in the same connected component have the same root
    components = {}
    for i in range(n):
        root = find(i)
        if root not in components:
            components[root] = []
        components[root].append(i)
    
    # Check if we can achieve sorted order
    # For each component, get the values in original and sorted at those positions
    # The values should match in multiset
    
    for root in components:
        original_values = [a[i] for i in components[root]]
        sorted_values = [sorted_a[i] for i in components[root]]
        
        # Check if multisets are equal
        if sorted(original_values) != sorted(sorted_values):
            print(0)
            return
    
    print(1)
```
