I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is a classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Coin Problem". Given a set of positive integers, we need to find the largest number that cannot be expressed as a sum of these numbers (where each number can be used unlimited times).

Key observations:
1. If the greatest common divisor (GCD) of all numbers in the list is greater than 1, then all sums will be multiples of that GCD. This means there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed (specifically, all numbers not divisible by the GCD).
2. If the GCD is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed (this is the Frobenius number).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Check if GCD > 1
- If GCD of all numbers > 1, then we cannot form all numbers (only multiples of GCD), so return -1 (infinite numbers cannot be formed)

### Step 2: Apply the Frobenius number formula
- For two coprime numbers a and b, the Frobenius number is a×b - a - b
- For more than two numbers, there's no simple formula, but we can use the fact that if the numbers are coprime, then all numbers greater than a certain value can be formed.

### Step 3: Find the Frobenius number for multiple numbers
- If all numbers have GCD 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed
- We can use dynamic programming to find it efficiently

## Algorithm Implementation

```python
import math
from functools import reduce

def survivor(zombies):
    # Handle edge cases
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    def gcd_of_list(lst):
        return reduce(math.gcd, lst)
    
    g = gcd_of_list(zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD is 1, then there is a largest number that cannot be formed
    # We'll use dynamic programming to find it
    
    # The Frobenius number for multiple coprime integers is not straightforward,
    # but we know that all sufficiently large numbers can be formed.
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be formed.
    
    # We'll use the fact that if we have a set of coprime numbers, 
    # the largest number that cannot be formed is bounded by the product of the numbers minus the sum.
    
    # For simplicity, we can use the fact that if the numbers are coprime,
    # we can find the largest non-representable number using DP.
    
    # Set a reasonable upper bound - the Frobenius number for coprime numbers
    # is at most (max(zombies) * min(zombies) - max(zombies) - min(zombies))
    # But for multiple numbers, we can use the bound: all numbers > (max(zombies) * min(zombies)) can be formed
    
    # Actually, a more reliable approach: we'll use the fact that if we have numbers with GCD 1,
    # there exists a finite Frobenius number, and we can find it using dynamic programming.
    
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be formed.
    # We'll use the fact that once we can form a sequence of consecutive numbers,
    # we can form all larger numbers by adding the smallest number.
    
    # Start with a reasonable bound - the Frobenius number is at most max(zombies) * min(zombies)
    # But to be safe, we can use the product of all numbers minus their sum
    # Actually, a standard bound is that all numbers > max(zombies) * min(zombies) can be formed
    # But let's use a practical approach with DP.
    
    # The Frobenius number for multiple coprime integers is unknown in general,
    # but we know that the largest number that cannot be formed is finite.
    
    # Use DP to find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We'll set a reasonable upper bound
    
    if len(zombies) == 1:
        # For a single number a, only multiples of a can be formed
        # So any number not divisible by a cannot be formed
        # Since GCD is 1, this would be a contradiction, so actually GCD must be 1
        # For a single number, if GCD is 1, then a=1
        # So if we have [1], then every number can be formed, so no number cannot be formed
        # Actually, if we have [1], then 1 can be formed, 2=1+1, etc.
        # So there is no number that cannot be formed
        return 0
    
    # For multiple numbers with GCD 1, find the largest non-representable number
    # Use the bound: all numbers > (max(zombies) * min(zombies)) can be formed (this is not strictly true)
    # Instead, we'll use the known result that the Frobenius number is bounded by the product
    
    # Set a reasonable upper bound - use the maximum possible value in the list
    # times the minimum, minus some value
    min_val = min(zombies)
    max_val = max(zombies)
    
    # Actually, a better approach is to use the fact that if we can form a sequence of min_val consecutive numbers,
    # then we can form all larger numbers by adding min_val.
    # The largest number that cannot be formed is at most min_val * max_val - min_val - max_val
    
    # But for general case with multiple numbers, we use the following:
    # The largest number that cannot be formed is finite and can be found by checking all values
    # up to a certain bound.
    
    # We'll use the fact that the answer is at most min_val * max_val
    # Actually, the known bound for Frobenius number with multiple coprime integers is not simple.
    
    # Simple practical approach: check each number starting from 1 until we find a sequence
    # of min_val consecutive numbers that can be formed.
    
    # We'll implement a simple DP solution
    # The bound we use: all numbers > max(zombies) * min(zombies) can be formed
    # But this is only true for two numbers.
    
    # For safety, we'll check up to (max_val * min_val) + 1
    bound = max_val * min_val
    
    # Create a boolean array to track which numbers can be formed
    can_form = [False] * (bound + 1)
    
    # Base case: 0 can be formed (using 0 of each number)
    can_form[0] = True
    
    # For each number in the list, we can form sums by adding that number
    for num in zombies:
        for i in range(num, bound + 1):
            can_form[i] = can_form[i] or can_form[i - num]
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
    
    # But we need to ensure we found the true maximum
    # In theory, if GCD is 1, there should be a maximum
    # However, our bound might be too small
    
    # Actually, let's reconsider: if GCD = 1, the Frobenius number exists and is finite,
    # but the exact value is complex. For practical purposes with inputs up to 1000,
    # and given that we need to return an answer, the above DP will work.
    
    # Check if there's actually a largest non-representable number
    # If all numbers >= some value can be formed, then we have our answer
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We'll iterate through all numbers up to bound
    max_non_formed = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            max_non_formed = i
    
    # If no such number found, return 0 (all numbers can be formed)
    # This happens when GCD > 1? No, we already handled GCD > 1
    # Actually, if GCD = 1, there should be at least one number that cannot be formed
    # But for single [1], everything can be formed, so answer is 0
    
    # Special case: if all numbers are 1, then every number can be formed
    if all(num == 1 for num in zombies):
        return 0
    
    return max_non_formed

# But wait - we need to simplify and make sure this works correctly.

def survivor(zombies):
    # Handle edge cases
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    g = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # This means there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed
    # For multiple numbers, we use a simple DP approach
    
    # The Frobenius number is finite when GCD is 1
    # We'll use a bound: all numbers > (max(zombies) * min(zombies)) can be formed
    # This is a known result for coprimes, but we'll use a practical bound
    
    if len(zombies) == 1:
        # If we have only one number, say a, and GCD=1, then a=1
        # Since 1 can form all numbers, there is no number that cannot be formed
        return 0
    
    # Set a reasonable upper bound
    min_val = min(zombies)
    max_val = max(zombies)
    bound = max_val * min_val  # This is a safe upper bound
    
    # DP array: can_form[i] is True if i can be formed
    can_form = [False] * (bound + 1)
    can_form[0] = True  # 0 can be formed with 0 of each number
    
    # For each zombie number, update the can_form array
    for num in zombies:
        for i in range(num, bound + 1):
            can_form[i] = can_form[i] or can_form[i - num]
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
    
    # Since GCD is 1, the sequence of representable numbers will eventually include
    # min_val consecutive numbers, and then all larger numbers can be formed by adding min_val
    # Therefore, the largest non-representable number is the maximum i where can_form[i] is False
    
    return result
```

```python
import math
from functools import reduce

def survivor(zombies):
    # Handle edge case: empty list
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    g = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # This means infinitely many numbers cannot be formed (all non-multiples)
    if g > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed
    # Special case: if all numbers are 1, every number can be formed
    if all(num == 1 for num in zombies):
        return 0
    
    # For multiple numbers with GCD 1, we find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We'll use dynamic programming with a reasonable bound
    
    # The Frobenius number for multiple coprime integers is bounded
    # We can use the fact that after a certain point, all numbers can be formed
    
    min_val = min(zombies)
    # Set bound - for accuracy, we use max_val * min_val as a reasonable upper bound
    bound = max(zombies) * min(zombies)
    
    # DP array: can_form[i] indicates if number i can be formed
    can_form = [False] * (bound + 1)
    can_form[0] = True  # 0 can be formed
    
    # For each zombie number, update the can_form array
    for num in zombies:
        for i in range(num, bound + 1):
            can_form[i] = can_form[i] or can_form[i - num]
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(1, bound + 1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
    
    return result
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is a classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Coin Problem" where we need to find the largest number that cannot be expressed as a sum of given numbers, with each number being used unlimited times.

Key observations:
- We have a list of positive natural numbers (the "zombies")
- We need to find the largest number that cannot be represented as a sum of these numbers
- If all numbers are greater than 1 and there's a "Frobenius number", we return that
- If the GCD of all numbers is greater than 1, then only multiples of that GCD can be formed, so there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
- If the GCD of all numbers is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed (Frobenius number)

## Mathematical Background

For two coprime numbers a and b, the largest number that cannot be expressed as ax + by (where x,y ≥ 0) is ab - a - b.

For more than two numbers, if they are coprime (GCD = 1), there exists a Frobenius number, but there's no simple formula. We need to find it algorithmically.

## Solution Steps

1. First, find the GCD of all numbers in the list
2. If GCD > 1, then only multiples of GCD can be formed, so there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed → return -1
3. If GCD = 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be formed. We need to find it.
4. For the case where GCD = 1, we can use the fact that once we can form a sequence of consecutive numbers, we can form all larger numbers by adding the smallest number.
5. We can use a dynamic programming approach to find the largest number that cannot be formed.

## Algorithm

1. Check GCD of all numbers:
   - If GCD > 1 → return -1
   - If GCD = 1 → proceed

2. Use dynamic programming to find the largest number that cannot be formed:
   - Create a boolean array to track which numbers can be formed
   - Starting from the smallest number in the list, mark all multiples as formable
   - Stop when we find a sequence of consecutive numbers (of length equal to the smallest number)

3. The answer is the largest unmarked number before we get a sequence of consecutive numbers.

```python
def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers in the list
    from math import gcd
    from functools import reduce
    
    total_gcd = reduce(gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, only multiples of GCD can be formed
    # So there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be formed
    if total_gcd > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, there exists a largest number that cannot be formed
    # Use the Frobenius coin problem solution
    
    # We need to find the largest number that cannot be represented
    # Once we have a sequence of k consecutive numbers where k is the smallest zombie number,
    # then all numbers larger than that can be formed by adding the smallest number
    
    # Start with the smallest number
    min_zombie = min(zombies)
    
    # We'll use dynamic programming to find which numbers can be formed
    # We only need to check up to min_zombie * min_zombie + min_zombie - 1
    # This is a known upper bound for the Frobenius number when GCD = 1
    
    # Maximum value we need to check
    # We can safely go up to min_zombie * min_zombie (this is an upper bound)
    max_check = min_zombie * min_zombie
    
    # dp[i] will be True if number i can be formed
    dp = [False] * (max_check + 1)
    
    # Base case: 0 can be formed (by adding nothing)
    dp[0] = True
    
    # For each number from 1 to max_check
    for i in range(1, max_check + 1):
        for zombie in zombies:
            if i >= zombie and dp[i - zombie]:
                dp[i] = True
                break
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(max_check + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            result = i
    
    # However, we need to ensure that after some point, all numbers can be formed
    # Check if we have a sequence of consecutive numbers of length min_zombie
    consecutive_count = 0
    for i in range(max_check + 1):
        if dp[i]:
            consecutive_count += 1
            if consecutive_count == min_zombie:
                break
    
    # If we've found min_zombie consecutive numbers, then all larger numbers can be formed
    # The largest number that cannot be formed is the last unmarked number before this sequence
    
    # Actually, we just need to return the largest unmarked number
    # But we need to make sure it's correct
    
    # If the numbers are coprime, there should be a largest unformable number
    # Find the maximum value that cannot be formed
    max_unformable = 0
    for i in range(max_check + 1):
        if not dp[i]:
            max_unformable = i
    
    return max_unformable
```

- 1: cannot be formed
- 2: cannot be formed  
- 3: can be formed (3)
- 4: can be formed (4)
- 5: cannot be formed (since 5-3=2, 5-4=1, both not formable)
- 6: 3+3 = 6 (can be formed)
- 7: 3+4 = 7 (can be formed)
- 8: 4+4 = 8 (can be formed)
- 9: 3+3+3 = 9 (can be formed)

```python
    min_zombie = min(zombies)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

This is the classic "Frobenius coin problem" or "Chicken McNugget theorem" - finding the largest number that cannot be expressed as a non-negative integer combination of given numbers.

Given a list of positive integers (the "zombies"), we need to find the largest natural number that cannot be represented as a sum of these numbers (where each number can be used unlimited times).

## Key Insights

1. **Frobenius Number**: For two coprime integers a and b, the largest number that cannot be expressed as ax + by (where x,y ≥ 0) is ab - a - b.

2. **General Case**: If the given numbers have a common divisor d > 1, then every sum will be divisible by d, so there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be represented (all numbers not divisible by d).

3. **When no such maximum exists**: If gcd of all numbers is greater than 1, then there are infinitely many numbers that cannot be represented (all numbers not divisible by the gcd).

4. **When a solution exists**: If gcd of all numbers is 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be represented (this is the Frobenius number for multiple variables).

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find the GCD of all numbers in the list
2. If GCD > 1, return -1 (infinite numbers cannot be represented)
3. If GCD = 1, then there exists a largest number that cannot be represented
4. For the case with multiple numbers, the Frobenius number is not known in general, but we can use the fact that once we can represent consecutive numbers of length equal to the smallest number, we can represent all larger numbers.

So the approach is:
- Find the GCD of all numbers. If > 1, return -1.
- If GCD = 1, then the answer is the largest number that cannot be represented.
- We can find this by checking each number until we find a sequence of consecutive numbers of length equal to the smallest number.

## Implementation Strategy

For the case where GCD = 1:
1. Sort the numbers
2. Find the smallest number (min_num)
3. Check numbers sequentially from 1 upwards
4. Keep track of which numbers can be represented
5. When we find min_num consecutive representable numbers, then all larger numbers can also be represented
6. The answer is the largest number that cannot be represented

```python
import math
from functools import reduce

def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    gcd_all = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be represented
    if gcd_all > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be represented
    # This is a known result in number theory - the Frobenius number
    
    # We'll use the approach: find the largest number that cannot be represented
    # by checking consecutive numbers until we have a run of length = min(zombies)
    
    zombies_set = set(zombies)
    min_zombie = min(zombies)
    
    # Use dynamic programming to find which numbers can be formed
    # We only need to check up to min_zombie * min_zombie or so
    # Since once we have min_zombie consecutive numbers, we can form all larger numbers
    
    # We'll check numbers starting from 1
    can_form = [False] * (min_zombie * min_zombie + 1)
    can_form[0] = True  # 0 can always be formed (using 0 of each)
    
    # Build all representable numbers up to a reasonable limit
    # We need to go until we have min_zombie consecutive numbers
    max_check = min_zombie * min_zombie
    
    for i in range(1, max_check + 1):
        if can_form[i]:
            for z in zombies:
                if i + z <= max_check:
                    can_form[i + z] = True
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    # We need to find the largest number that is not in can_form
    # Since we have consecutive numbers from some point, we can stop when we find min_zombie consecutive True values
    
    # Actually, we can find directly by scanning from the end
    result = 0
    for i in range(max_check, 0, -1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
            break
    
    return result
```

Wait, I need to refine this. The above approach works but might be inefficient for large inputs. Let me optimize it.

Actually, let me reconsider. The standard solution for this problem:

1. If GCD > 1, return -1
2. If GCD = 1, the largest number that cannot be formed is found by checking consecutive representable numbers

```python
import math
from functools import reduce

def survivor(zombies):
    if not zombies:
        return 0
    
    # Find GCD of all numbers
    gcd_all = reduce(math.gcd, zombies)
    
    # If GCD > 1, then infinitely many numbers cannot be represented
    if gcd_all > 1:
        return -1
    
    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be represented
    # Use the fact that once we have a sequence of consecutive numbers of length = min(zombies),
    # we can represent all larger numbers.
    
    min_zombie = min(zombies)
    
    # We'll use a set of representable numbers
    # We only need to go up to min_zombie * min_zombie or so
    # Let's go up to min_zombie * min_zombie for safety
    
    # Use dynamic programming
    can_form = [False] * (min_zombie * min_zombie + 1)
    can_form[0] = True
    
    # Generate all possible sums
    for i in range(len(can_form)):
        if can_form[i]:
            for z in zombies:
                if i + z < len(can_form):
                    can_form[i + z] = True
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(len(can_form) - 1, -1, -1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
            break
    
    return result
```

    if gcd_all > 1:

    # If GCD = 1, find the largest number that cannot be represented
    # Use the fact that once we have a sequence of consecutive numbers of length = min(zombies),
    # we can represent all larger numbers.
    
    min_zombie = min(zombies)
    
    # We'll use a set of representable numbers
    # We only need to go up to min_zombie * min_zombie or so
    # Let's go up to min_zombie * min_zombie for safety
    
    # Use dynamic programming
    can_form = [False] * (min_zombie * min_zombie + 1)
    can_form[0] = True
    
    # Generate all possible sums
    for i in range(len(can_form)):
        if can_form[i]:
            for z in zombies:
                if i + z < len(can_form):
                    can_form[i + z] = True
    
    # Find the largest number that cannot be formed
    result = 0
    for i in range(len(can_form) - 1, -1, -1):
        if not can_form[i]:
            result = i
            break
    
    return result
